<div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-433"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="433" data-folio="414" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n432_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 414">[p. 414]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1">II.</h2><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">CONDITION OF INDIA.</span></h3><h4 class="sec-h3">(Correspondence with the Secretary of State for India, 1880.)</h4><h4 class="sec-h3">I.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">32, Great St. Helens, London, 24th May 1880.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">To The Right Honourable THE MARQUIS OF HARTINGTON, The Secretary of State for India, India Office.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">My Lord,</span> I beg to submit a series of tables, working out in detail the total production of the Punjab, for the year 1876-7. My objects in troubling your Lordship are as follows:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In 1876 I read some papers on the Poverty of India before the Bombay Branch of the East India Association of London. These papers are published in the Journals of the Association, and I send herewith a copy. At pages 237 to 239<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-22-fn-001" href="#sec-22-fn-001" data-fn-target="sec-22-fn-001" role="doc-noteref">*</a></sup> I have explained how the mode of taking the averages adopted in the various Administration Reports of India, was quite wrong. When preparing my Papers on the Poverty of India, I had not sufficient time to work out all the averages for all the Provinces in detail. I have now worked out in detail the averages of all the production Tables of the Administration Report of the Punjab for 1876-7. I request now that the different Governments in India may be directed to supply their tables of production, as fully as are prescribed by the Statistical Committee of Calcutta, that the averages may be correctly taken, as I have done in the enclosed tables, and that in addition to the tables prescribed, may also be given a summary of the total agricultural production like the one given at page 21 of my tables, a summary of the whole production (agricultural, manufactures and mines) like that at page 23, and a table of the absolute necessaries of life for an agricultural labourer, like that at pages 26 to 28.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It is only when such complete information is furnished by the Indian authorities, that any true conception can be formed of the actual material condition of India from year to year, and our British rulers can only then clearly see, and grapple with effectually, the important problem of the material condition of India, and the best means of improving it.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have also to solicit your Lordship to submit my tables to the Statistical Department of the India Office, and to direct to oblige me by pointing out any mistakes of facts or figures there may be in them.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-434"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="434" data-folio="415" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n433_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 415">[p. 415]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In troubling your Lordship with these requests, I have no other object than to help, as far as my humble opportunities go, to arrive at the real truth of the actual material condition of India. For it is only natural that without the knowledge of the whole truth on this most important subject, all efforts, however well and benevolently intentioned, must generally result in disappointment and failures.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I also earnestly desire and solicit that your Lordship will kindly take into your consideration the representations I have urged in my papers on the Poverty of India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remanin,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">My Lord,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Your Lordship’s most obedient Servant,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">DADABHAI NAOROJI</span>.</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1">ADMINISTRATION REPORT OF PUNJAB 1876-7.</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Page 77, “Upon the whole the character of the weather during the year 1876-7 was favourable for agriculture.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have taken 1 seer equal 2·057 lbs. from the Compilation entitled “Prices of food grains throughout India—1861-76, compiled in the Financial Department of the Government of India—Calcutta, 1878.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The prices I have adopted are the average of the prices given in the Report for 1st January 1876, 1st June 1876, and 1st January 1877— the last being the latest price that is given in the Report.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For all such particulars or figures as are <em>not</em> given in the Report, I have consulted a Punjab farmer, and adopted such information as he has given me.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There are some figures in the report which are evidently some mistakes and are much in excess of probability. But I have not altered them, though by retaining them as given in the report, the quantity and value of some of the articles become much higher than what they must most probably really be.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Excepting such mistakes, the farmer thinks the tables of the Report give a fair representation of the produce of Punjab—the averages being worked out in the right way they should be, and not as they are given in the Report, worked on a wrong principle.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-435"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="435" data-folio="416" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n434_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 416">[p. The quantity will then become lbs. 541,492,369 and value Rs. 2,65,12,580. Again the price of rice given in the Report is for “1st sort” only. The medium or 2nd sort forms the bulk and there is lower sort still. 2nd sort is generally about 75 per cent. of the 1st sort. I take upon the whole 85 per cent. The value then for the whole bulk will be Rs. 2,25,35,693.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-436"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="436" data-folio="417" The quantity will be lbs. 5,332,813,517, and value will be Rupees 9,97,20,021. The price given in the report is for 1st sort only. The 2nd sort forms the bulk, and is generally about 12 per cent. lower in price. I take only 8 per cent. lower for the whole bulk.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The value of the whole will then be Rs. 9,17,42,419.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" 422]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It should be noted that the prices of Jowár, Bájrá, Másh, Mung and Arhar are nearly the same generally, but of the remaining 5 kinds of grain, viz. Moth, Kangni, China, Matter and Masur, the prices are generally nearly 25 per cent. lower. The prices I have used in the table are as given in the Report for Jáwar and Bájrá only—though the acreage of the lower priced grains is 1,409,893 acres out of 6,534,963 acres, or above 20 per cent. If the allowance for the lower price of the 5 kinds of grains mentioned above were made, the value will evidently be much lower than I have given above. It requires also to be noted that out of the inferior grains a portion goes for the feed of animals in about the following proportions:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-110"><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Government pay Rs. 5 per seer or Rs. 2½ per lb. to the producer. The total value will therefore be Rs. 3,86,375.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The additional value at which Government sells opium is a part of the national income as it is chiefly paid by a Foreign Country as profit of trade, and therefore (as I have done in my “Poverty of India”) the net opium Revenue will have to be added to the total production of the country. The particular provinces only from which this revenue is derived, viz. Bengal, Bombay and other opium producing places, cann ot be credited with this income. It belongs to the whole nation, as every place is not quite free to cultivate opium.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-443"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="443" data-folio="424" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n442_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 424">[p. 424]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Tobacco</span>.</h3><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-113"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-113 · FOLIO 443</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tables/tbl-113_p0443.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tidy/tbl-113_p0443.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><span class="sc">Tobacco</span>.</caption><thead><tr><th scope="col">Districts.</th><th scope="col">Acres.</th><th scope="col">Per acre.</th><th scope="col">Total quantity.</th><th scope="col">Price per Re. I have, as above, kept the Report’s price, though it is considered so high.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-444"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="444" data-folio="425" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n443_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 425">[p. 425]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Turmeric.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Neither produce per acre, nor price is given in the Report. The price is taken say 10 ℔s. per 1 Rupee.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">4130 acres × 164 ℔s. = 677,320 ℔s.—at ℔s. 10 per rupee = Rs. 67,732.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Coriander Seed.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As above neither produce per acre nor price is given in the Report. evidently too high. 80 lbs. will be nearer the mark. If so, the above quantity and value are nearly 36½ per cent. above the right quantity and value.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Very probably some of the figures of produce per acre are for uncleaned or seed cotton. The report uses the word “cotton” only in the column of produce per acre, while in the column for prices it uses the words “Cotton (cleaned).”</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-447"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="447" data-folio="428" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n446_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 428">[p. 428]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Hemp</span>.</h3><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-117"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-117 · FOLIO 447</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tables/tbl-117_p0447.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tidy/tbl-117_p0447.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><span class="sc">Hemp</span>.</caption><thead><tr><th scope="col">Districts.</th><th scope="col">Acres.</th><th scope="col">Per Acre.</th><th scope="col">Total quantity.</th><th scope="col">Price lbs. per Re. 1.</th><th scope="col">Total value Rs.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">lbs.</th><th scope="col">lbs.</th><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col"></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1</td><td class="num">2,100</td><td class="num"><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-22-fn-011" href="#sec-22-fn-011" data-fn-target="sec-22-fn-011" role="doc-noteref">*</a></sup> 1,158</td><td class="num">2,431,800</td><td colspan="2" rowspan="23">The prices are not given in the Report. of “Fibres;” and as produce per acre of cotton is given separately, the produce per acre under the heading “Fibres” applies to Hemp.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-448"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="448" data-folio="429" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n447_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 429">[p. 429]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Kassamba (Safflower)</span>.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Neither produce per acre nor price is given in the Report. I take 40 lbs. per acre of dry prepared stuff—and price 2½ lbs. per Re. 1. Acres 24,708 by 40 lbs. = 988,320 at 2½ lbs. per Re. 1, gives Rs. 3,95,328.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Indigo</span>.</h4><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-118"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-118 · FOLIO 448</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tables/tbl-118_p0448.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tidy/tbl-118_p0448.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><span class="sc">Indigo</span>.</caption><thead><tr><th scope="col">Districts.</th><th scope="col">Acres.</th><th scope="col">Per Acre.</th><th scope="col">Total quantity.</th><th scope="col">Price lbs. per Re. 1.</th><th scope="col">Total value Rs.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">lbs.</th><th scope="col">lbs.</th><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col"></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">1</td><td class="num">100</td><td class="num">30</td><td class="num">3,000</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">56</td><td class="num">100</td><td class="num">5,600</td><td colspan="2" rowspan="17">The price is not given in the Report. 1,167,948,561</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The prices I have taken above are given in the Report for potato only—and the average comes to say 31 lbs. per Re. 1. This is a high average price. The average price of potato will be nearer 60 than 31 lbs. I take however the average of 31 lbs.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-450"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="450" data-folio="431" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n449_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 431">[p. 431]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Now out of the vegetables grown, about one-eighth only will be potato and seven-eighths other kind of general vegetables.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This will give, out of lbs. 1,220,573,777—seven-eighths of general vegetables=lbs. 1,068,002,055.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The price of vegetables is not given in the Report. It may be taken as 1½ maunds per Re. 1 or 124 lbs., say 100 lbs. per Re. 1—which will give the total value of vegetables to be about Rs. 1,06,80,020.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Again the average of 4,753 lbs. is of vegetables, but potato will be only about 30 maunds or lbs. 2,460 per acre, and as potato will be about ⅛th of the acreage planted with vegetables, or about 32,100 acres, the total quantity of potato will be 32,100 × 2,460=78,966,000 lbs. This at the price of 31 lbs. per Re. 1 will give Rs. 25,47,290.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I make no deduction for seed potato, or seed for vegetables.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Tea.</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The produce per acre is given for 1 district only. But the Report at page 78 takes the general average to be the same, viz. 96 lbs. The price is not given. I take 3 lbs. per Re. 1.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-120"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-120 · FOLIO 450</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a id="page-452"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="452" data-folio="433" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n451_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 433">[p. 433]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The average price, as obtained on the basis of the prices given in the Report, is, for “1st sort” or what is called Misri. But there are different qualities of sugar, viz. Gol, Red Sugar, ordinary 2nd sort sugar, and best or 1st sort sugar. Taking the price of 1st sort as averaging 6 lbs. per Rupee, the prices of the other kinds are:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Gol … … 24 lbs. per Rupee ) Of these the first two form nearly Red Sugar … 16 ,, ,, } <sup>2</sup>/<sub>3</sub>rds and the last two form <sup>1</sup>/<sub>3</sub>rd Ordinary 2nd 7 ,, ,, } of the whole quantity. 1st sort … 6 ,, ,, ) Taking in this ratio we get <sup>2</sup>/<sub>3</sub>rds at 20 lbs. = 13 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>3</sub> } or 15 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> or say 15 lbs. per Rupee. <sup>1</sup>/<sub>3</sub>rd „ 6 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> „ = 2 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>6</sub> }</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The whole quantity, being 253,012,132 lbs., will at 15 lbs. per Rupee give the total value Rs. 1,68,67,475.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For seed, to deduct cane equal to 40 lbs. of sugar per acre.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This gives 16 fold, and taking the higher average of 646 lbs., I deduct say 6 per cent.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The whole quantity is then lbs. 237,831,405.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">And the whole value is then Rs. 1,58,55,427.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">If, as I have pointed out above, the average of Delhi (1) were taken 500 lbs. instead of 1,500 lbs., which would make the average produce of the whole of the Punjab 487 lbs. instead of 646, the above quantity and value will prove some 30 per cent. higher than they should be.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It may be noted here, that the Report itself makes the average 449 lbs. only, on the fallacious principle of simply adding up and dividing by the number of districts, while when properly calculated the figure should be 646 instead of 449. This is an instance of how misleading and incorrect the averages are as they are generally calculated in the <strong>Administration Reports.</strong></p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-453"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="453" data-folio="434" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n452_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 434">[p. 434]</button></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-122"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-122 · FOLIO 453</span><span class="folio-trigger" data-page="455" data-folio="436" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n454_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 436">[p. 436]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Mines.</span></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There is no clear statement of the value of the produce of Mines given in this Report. The chief article is Salt. The report does not give any account of the cost of Salt.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Parl. Return No. 176 of 1878 gives (page 30) “The quantity manufactured, excavated or purchased” during the year (1876-77) as Maunds 1,795,956. In the statistics published by the Government of India (1875) at Calcutta—part III., page 79, it is said “Since 4th July 1870, one anna per maund has been charged as the cost price of the salt in addition to the duty.” At this rate the above production of Salt, viz. Maunds 1,795,956 will cost Rs. 1,12,247. Duty is paid from the produce of the country.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For other minerals I can get no estimate. I roughly and as a very outside estimate put down the <em>whole</em> product of mines at Rs. 3 lacs.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Stock.</span></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am unable to make any estimate of the annual addition to stock during the year. All that portion however which is used for agricultural or manufacturing purposes need not be estimated, as its labour, like that of the agriculturer and the manufacturer himself, is included in the agricultural or manufacturing produce. The portion of the annual produce or addition, which is used for other than agricultural and manufacturing purposes, such as carriage and food and milk, needs to be added to the production of the year. Though I cannot estimate this, still it will not matter much, for, as I have shown in the table for inferior grains, a certain portion of them goes in the feed of animals— and as this portion supplies the feed of the <em>whole</em> stock that requires grain, and not merely that of the <em>annual</em> addition, the non-estimate of that portion of the <em>annual</em> addition to the stock which is used for carriage and for food may be more than covered by the value of the grain used for animals. Moreover, as I also give a margin upon the total estimate for any omission, any such item will be fully provided for.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-124"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-124 · FOLIO 455</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tables/tbl-124_p0455.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tidy/tbl-124_p0455.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption>Summary of the total production of Punjab.</caption><thead><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Value.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Agricultural Produce</td><td class="num">Rs. 27,72,56,263</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Manufactures</td><td>" 4,08,40,058</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Mines</td><td>" 3,00,000</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td></td><td class="num">Rs. 31,83,96,321</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-456"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="456" data-folio="437" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n455_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 437">[p. 437]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In order to meet any omissions (fish, &amp;c.) I allow a further margin of above 3<span class="sc">½</span> crores of Rupees, making say, the whole produce of Punjab 35<span class="sc">½</span> crores of Rupees, or at 2<em>s.</em> per Re. = £35,330,000, which, for a population of 17,600,000, gives £2 per head per annum at the outside for the year 1876-7.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The approximate estimate I had made out for the year 1867-8 in my paper on The Poverty of India was 49<em>s.</em> 5<em>d.</em>, showing that either my calculation for the year 1867-8 was too high or the production of the province has diminished in value. The truth most likely is between both.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">At all events unless any error of importance is pointed out, it seems clearly established that the value of the production of one of the best Provinces of India is Rs. 20 per head per annum at the outside.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Food Produce</span>.</h4><div class="ledger-table-block" taking the <em>whole</em> quantity of grain as for human use and thus not allowing any portion at all for animals (which would of course not be right to do) the quantity per annum will be 735 lbs. or 2 lbs. per day per head.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the value I have calculated for grain, I have taken the <em>whole</em> grain, <em>i. e.</em> including the portion for animals.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Vegetables</span>.</h4><h4 class="sec-h3">General Vegetables.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Total quantity 1,068,002,055 lbs. Deducting tobacco, it will be Rs. 34-2 as., say Rs. 32.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">2 More Members in a Family.</span></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1 Young person, say between 12 and 18—Say Rs. 26—though there will not be so much difference,</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1 “” under 12 Say “0 though this cannot be the case generally.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-459"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="459" data-folio="440" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n458_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 440">[p. 440]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2">Cost of absolute Necessaries of Life of an agricultural labourer— (<em>continued</em>).</h3><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-128"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-128 · FOLIO 459</span><span Rs. 6—for the 2nd, say nothing.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-129"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-129 · FOLIO 459</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tables/tbl-129_p0459.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tidy/tbl-129_p0459.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption>FAMILY EXPENSES IN COMMON.</caption><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Cottage, Rs. 60 Say Rs. 4 0 for 1 year.</td><td>Calculated on</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Repairs ... ... ... 3 0</td><td>the lowest scale</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Cooking and other utensils. 3 8</td><td>without any fur-</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Firewood, ½ anna per day. 5 11</td><td>niture—such as</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Lamp oil, 1 oz. per day, at 3 seers per Re. 1 ... ... 3 12</td><td>cots or mats, or stools or any</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td></td><td>thing.</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td>Total... 19 15</td><td></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-130"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-130 · FOLIO 459</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tables/tbl-130_p0459.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tidy/tbl-130_p0459.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption>Taking 4 in the family.</caption><thead><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Food.</th><th scope="col">Clothing.</th><th scope="col">Family expenses.</th><th scope="col">Total.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Rs.</th><th scope="col">Rs. a.</th><th scope="col">Rs. a.</th><th scope="col"></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Man ... ... ...</td><td class="num">37</td><td>10 4</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Woman ... ...</td><td class="num">32</td><td>6 11</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Youth (12 to 18) ...</td><td class="num">26</td><td>6 0</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Child (under 12) ...</td><td>...</td><td>......</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td></td><td class="num">95</td><td>22 15</td><td>19 15</td><td>137 14—say Rs. 136.</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Which will be Rs. 34 per head per annum in a family of 4—against the production of Rs. 20 per annum at the outside. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-460" data-page="460" data-folio="441" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n459_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 441">[p. 441]</button> No wedding, birth, and funeral expenses calculated, nor medical, educational, social and religious wants, but simply the absolute necessaries for existence in ordinary health, at the lowest scale of cost and quantity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The prices this year are the lowest during 10 years.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Report says—page 83—“Salt and tobacco show a rise in price.” This is a mistake into which the writer is led by the mistake of the clerk in taking his totals and division by the number of districts. The figures in table 45 (page clxxvii) in the line of the “General Average” of tobacco <em>viz.</em> 4-5 and 5-7 are wrong. And so also in the line of Salt 7 and 7-5 are wrong. I do not mean these figures are wrong on account of the fallacious principle of the report in taking averages, but in taking the average according to the report’s own method, <em>i. e.</em> of adding up the columns and dividing by the number of districts.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">2</h4><h4 class="sec-h3"><em>Memorandum on Mr. Danvers’s papers of 28th June 1880 and 4th January 1879.</em></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Danvers says:—“In examining Mr. Dadabhai Naoroji’s paper, it appears that in his calculations he has omitted to make any allowance for the value of straw, and he has made no attempt to estimate the value of the increase of Agricultural stock, but he has added an arbitrary sum for the latter and for other omitted items.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have omitted not only straw, but also Grass, Cotton seed, and any fodder or other food for animals which I have not taken in my tables; and further, I should also omit all that portion of the inferior grains which I have shown in my table at page 9—of about 30 per cent. of the whole acreage of grains, and which is grown for the food of animals.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The reason is this. The principle to be considered is:—1st—Either the whole gross annual production of the country may be taken (including straw, grass, &amp;c. &amp;c.) and from this <em>gross</em> production, before apportioning it per head of human population, a deduction should be made for the portion required for all the stock—which in the case of the Punjab are above 7,000,000 large cattle and near 4,000,000 sheep and goats; or 2nd —All straw, grass and every production raised for animal food should be left out of calculation, and only the rest of the production which is and can be turned to human use, should be apportioned among the human population. Mr. Danvers may adopt either of the above two methods —whichever he may consider would give most correctly the actual production for human use. It would not be correct to include the produce <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-461" data-page="461" data-folio="442" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n460_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 442">[p. 442]</button> raised for animal use and then not to make the necessary deduction for such use. I would put this matter in another form.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Suppose on the 1st of Janaury 1880, we have in India a certain amount of material wealth in all its various forms, and we take complete stock of it; that during the year following the country works in all its varieties of ways, consumes for all its various human, animal and instrumental wants from the store existing ou the 1st January 1880; and that after the end of the year, on 1st January 1881, we gather together or take stock of every possible kind of material production (agricultural, mineral and manufacturing and addition from profits of foreign trade) during the year: ‘This production during the year will have to meet all the wants of the next year. If this production prove less than what would be wanted for the next year, then there would be a deficiency, and either the original wealth or capital of the country will have to be drawn upon, or the people will be so much less supplied with their wants in some shape or other; in either way showing a diminution of prosperity—both as property and capacity. If on the other hand, the whole material production of the year prove more than what would be necessary for the next year for all ordinary or usual wants, then a surplus would accrue, and so far in some permanent form, add to the capital of the country and increase its prosperity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I request therefore that Mr. Danvers may be asked to work out the total production and wants of India, for say the last dozen years, on correct principles of calculations, from such materials as are already available at the India Office, supplementing such information as may be deficient by asking from India and from experienced retired officials who are now in this country. Such tables will show what the actual material condition of the country is, and whether it is increasing or diminishing in prosperity. Unless such information is obtained, the Government of the country will be blind and in the dark, and cannot but result in misery to India, and discredit to the Rulers, their best intentions notwithstanding. It is hopeless to expect intelligent government without the aid of such important information annually.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am glad Mr. Danvers has made an estimate of the annual increase of agricultural stock in his paper of 4th January 1879, and as I have to say something upon this paper further on, I do not say anything here upon the subject of stock.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Danvers says:—“Mr. Dadabhai has adopted the principle of equally apportioning the value of agricultural produce and manufactures <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-462" data-page="462" data-folio="443" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n461_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 443">[p. 443]</button> as ascertained by him from the statistics available, amongst the whole population, without distinguishing how many are agriculturists, how many mechanics, and how many belong to other trades or professions, or possess property, and whose incomes therefore are derived directly neither from agriculture nor from manufactures. Thus he omits all reference to railway wealth, Government stock, house property, profits of trade, salaries, pensions, non-agricultural wages, professional incomes, and returns to investments, and all other sources from which a man who does not grow food himself may obtain the means of purchasing it.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“From the Census Report of 1871 it appears that, out of a total population of 17,611,498 under British Administration, in the Punjab 9,689,650 are returned as agriculturists, 1,776,786, adult males, equivalent to about 4,500,000 of population as engaged in industrial occupations, thus leaving a population of nearly 3½ millions directly dependent neither upon agriculture, manufactures, nor mining, and who must therefore derive their means of subsistence from other sources.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I take each of the items;—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1st—“Railway wealth.” I am not sure what Mr. Danvers means by ‘Railway wealth.’ In his paper of 4th January 1879 he regards railways “enhancing the value of food grains and adding, <em>protanto</em>, to the wealth of the districts through which they run.” If he means in the above extract, by “railway wealth” something different, then that needs to be explained. In the mean time I adopt the interpretation as I make out with the aid of his paper of 4th January 1879.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Suppose 100 maunds of wheat exist in the Punjab, and its cost to the producer, say, is Rs. 100—suppose that this wheat is carried by railway to Bombay and its value at Bombay is Rs. 125. Does Mr. Danvers mean that this circumstance has <em>added</em> Rs. 25 or anything at all to the existing wealth of India?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">If so, then no such thing has happened. The 100 maunds of wheat existed in the Punjab, and the Rs. 125 existed in Bombay, before the wheat was moved an inch. After the movement, the only result has been change of hands. The wheat has gone to Bombay and the Rs. 125 are distributed between the owner at Punjab, who receives Rs. 100, and the railway owners and workers, and the merchant who carried through the transaction, who between them divide the Rs. 25. By the mere fact of the removal of the wheat from the Punjab to Bombay, not a single grain of wheat nor a single pie of money is <em>added</em> to what already existed in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-463" data-page="463" data-folio="444" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n462_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 444">[p. 444]</button> India before the wheat was touched. Such “railway wealth” does not exist. If the mere movement of produce can <em>add</em> to the existing wealth, India can become rich in no time. All it would have to do, is to go on moving its produce continually all over India, all the year round, and under the magic wheels of the train, wealth will go on springing, till the land will not suffice to hold it. But there is no Royal (even railway) road to material wealth. It must be produced from the materials of the Earth till the great discovery is made of converting motion into matter. I should not be misunderstood. I am not discussing here the benefits of railways, whatever they are, to any country or to India. To show that the people of India are not deriving the usual benefits of railways, I give hereafter a short separate section. Here it is enough for me to state that railways are in a way an indirect means of increasing the material production of any country, but that whatever that “means” is, its result is fully and completely included in the estimate of the actual annual production of the country, and that there is nothing more to be <em>added</em> to such actual material production of the year.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">2nd—“Government Stock.” Suppose I hold a lakh of rupees of Government 4 per cent. Rupee paper. It does not from itself produce or create or make to grow out any money or food or any kind of material wealth for me. It simply means that Government will give me Rs. 4,000 every year, and that, not by creating anything by any divine power, but from the revenue of the country; and this revenue can be got from only the actual material production of the year. So in reality my income of Rs. 4,000 from “Government Stock” is nothing more nor less than a share out of the production of the country, and is therefore fully and completely included therein. No addition has to be made from “Government Stock” to the actual material production of the year. No such addition exists at all.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">3rd—“House Property.” Suppose I have taken a house at a yearly rent of Rs. 1,000. The house does not grow or create the rent by the mere fact of my occupying it. I have to pay this amount out of my income of the Rs. 4,000 from Government Stock, and so the house-owner receives through me and the Government his share out of the production of the country. The discussion of the other items further on will show that, be my income from any of the various sources Mr. Danvers suggests, it is ultimately and solely derived from and is included in, the yearly production of the country, and the owners of “House Property” simply take their share, like everybody else, from this same store.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-464"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="464" data-folio="445" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n463_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 445">[p. 445]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><strong>4th</strong>—“<strong>Profits of Trade.</strong>” I take first foreign trade. Mr. Danvers is quite right that the foreign trade of a country adds to its annual income or production.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-22-fn-019" href="#sec-22-fn-019" data-fn-target="sec-22-fn-019" role="doc-noteref">*</a></sup> But unfortunately the case with India is quite otherwise. The present system of British Administration not only sweeps away to England the whole profits of the foreign trade, but also drains away a portion of the annual production itself of the country. So India, instead of making <em>any</em> addition from its “profits of foreign trade” to its yearly production, a deduction has to be made from such production in estimating the actual quantity that ultimately remains for the use of the people of India. A portion of the actual production, through the channel of foreign trade, goes clean out of the country to England, without an atom of material return. The manner in which the foreign trade of India becomes the channel through which India’s present greatest misfortune and evil operate, I treat further on in a separate section, to avoid confusion. It is enough for me to say here, that as matters actually stand, instead of there being, as should be, any addition from foreign trade to the annual production of India, there is actually a diminution or drain of it, clean out of the country to England, to the extent of some £18,000,000 a year, together with and over and above all its “profits of trade.” I grieve, therefore, that I have nothing to <em>add</em> from “profits of trade” as Mr. Danvers suggests, but much to <em>subtract</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I take next the internal trade. Resuming the illustration of the 100 maunds of wheat at Punjab, say a merchant buys at Rs. 100 and sends it to Bombay, where he gets Rs. 125. The result simply is, that the wheat is still the same 100 maunds, and the Rs. 125 that existed in Bombay are still Rs. 125, but that out of Rs. 25, the merchant receives his “profit of trade,” and the railway its charges for carrying. Not a single atom of money or wheat is added to the existing wealth of the country by this internal trade; only a different distribution has taken place. I should not be misunderstood. I am not discussing here the usefulness of internal trade whatever it is. I am only pointing out that any increase in the material income of the country by the mere transactions of the internal trade, is a thing that does not exist, and that whatever benefits and “profits of trade” there are from internal trade, are fully and completely included in the ultimate result of the actual material production of the year.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-465"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="465" data-folio="446" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n464_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 446">[p. 446]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">5th—<strong>“Salaries and pensions.”</strong> These will be official and non-official. Official salaries and pensions are paid by Government from revenue, and this revenue is derived from the production of the country; and so from that same store are all such salaries and pensions derived. For non-official salaries or pensions, the phenomenon is just the same. I pay my clerks or servants, either from my profits of trade, or interest of Government stock, or from rent of my house property, or from any of the sources which Mr. Danvers may suggest, but one and all of these incomes are drawn from the same store,—the annual material production of the country. All salaries and pensions are thus fully and completely included in the estimate of the production.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But this is not all. In these salaries and pensions, &amp;c. do we come to the very source of India’s chief misfortune and evil, which, as I have already said, works through the medium of the foreign trade. It is the salaries and pensions, and all other expenditure incident to the excessive European Agency, both in England and India, which is India’s chief curse, in the shape of its causing the exhausting drain which is destroying India. In the ordinary and normal circumstances of a country when all the salaries, pensions, &amp;c. are earned by the people themselves, and remain in the country itself to fructify in the people’s own pockets, there is no such thing as an addition to the annual production of the country from “salaries and pensions.” But as far as India is concerned, the case is much worse. All salaries and pensions &amp;c. paid to Europeans in England and India beyond the absolute necessity of the maintenance or supervision of British rule, are actually, first, a direct deprivation of the natural provision for similar classes of the people of the country, and second, a drain from the property and capacity of the country at large. So, unfortunately, is there nothing to be <em>added</em>, as Mr. Danvers asks, from “salaries and pensions,” but much to be <em>subtracted</em>, that is, either spent in England or remitted to England from the resources of India, and for which not a particle returns, and what is enjoyed in India itself by the Europeans.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Danvers may kindly consider his own salary. It is derived from the production of India. It is brought to England and not a farthing out of it returns to India. Even if it returned, it would be no <em>addition</em> to the wealth of India, but as it does not return, it is so much actual <em>diminution</em> from the means of the subsistence of the people. I should not be misunderstood. That for a good long time, a reasonable amount of payment for British rule is necessary for the regeneration of India, is true, and no thinking native of India denies this It is the evil <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-466" data-page="466" data-folio="447" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n465_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 447">[p. 447]</button> of excessive payment that India has to complain of. But what I have to point out here is that salaries and pensions, even to the natives themselves, are no addition to the wealth, aud much less are those which are not paid to the people of the country. The increase supposed by Mr. Danvers does not exist. There is, on the contrary, much diminution.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">6.th—“Non-agricultural wages.”</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A person, employed by a farmer, say as a labourer, upon building his house, is paid from the farmer’s agricultural income. A person employed by a merchant, a house holder, a stock holder, a pensioner, or a salaried man, or on a railway, is paid from their income, which, as I have explained, is derived from the only great store—the annual material production of the country. In short every labourer—mental or physical—has his share for his subsistence, through various channels, from the only one fountain-head,—the annual material production of the country. There is no source, outside the production (including any addition to it from profits of foreign trade) from which any individual derives his means of subsistence.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">7.th—“Professional incomes.”</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I consult a doctor or a solicitor. The mere act of my consulting these professional gentlemen does not enable me to create money to pay them. I must pay them from my income as an agriculturist, or a miner, or a manufacturer, or a stockholder, or a householder, &amp;c. &amp;c., and my such income is all and solely derived from the material production of the country.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I need not now go any further into a repetition of the same argument with regard to.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">8th.—“Returns to investments, and all other sources from which a man who does not grow food himself may obtain the means of purchasing it;” or leaving a population “directly dependent neither upon agriculture, manufactures, nor mining, and who must therefore derive their means of subsistence from other sources.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>There do not exist</em> any such “other sources,” except profits of foreign trade. But unfortunately for India, instead of foreign trade bringing any profits, it is actually the channel by which, in addition to all such profits, a portion of the production itself is also swept away. So India exhibits the strange phenomenon, that her people cannot get any benefit from profits of foreign trade, and cannot enjoy for their subsistence <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-467" data-page="467" data-folio="448" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n466_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 448">[p. 448]</button> even their own production fully or adequately. The result of all the different influences, forces, labour, knowledge, land, climate, railways or all other kinds of public works, good government, justice, security of property, law, order, and all the above 8 and other so-called sources of income, <em>is fully and completely</em> comprised in the <em>ultimate resultant</em> of all of them, viz. the actual material income of the year. Its increase or decrease every year, is in fact <em>the</em> test of the ultimate and full result of all the above direct and indirect means of the production of a country. If the material income of the year does not suffice for all the wants of the whole people for the year, the existing “capital” wealth of the country is drawn upon, and so far the capital and the capacity for annual production are diminished.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I submit therefore that Mr. Danvers’ argument of the “other sources” has to be laid aside.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Danvers says, “Mr. Dadabhai makes out the total value of the agricultural produce of the Punjab to be Rs. 27,72,56,263, and that from manufactures and mines Rs. 4,11,40,058. To this he adds, to meet any omissions, a further margin of 3½ crores, making the whole produce of the Punjab 35¼ crores of Rupees, “which for a population of 17,600,000 gives Rs. 20 per head per annum at the outside for the year 1876-77,” to which year the figures he has taken refer. At page 27 of his tables he shows that the cost of absolute necessaries of life of an agricultural labourer is Rs. 34 per annum, but he omits to explain how, under these circumstances, the people of the Punjab managed to live, and leaves the reader to draw his own conclusions how, with only Rs. 20 per annum, he can provide for an expenditure of Rs. 34.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Why, that is the very question I want government to answer,—how they can expect people to manage to live, under such circumstances, without continuously sinking in poverty. The first real question is,— are these facts or not? If not, then what are the actual facts of the “means and wants” of the people of India? If they are, then the question is for Mr. Danvers and government to answer how people can manage to live. The answer to the question is however obvious, viz that as the balance of income every year, available for the use of the people of India, does not suffice for the wants of the year, the capital wealth of the country is being drawn upon, and the country goes on becoming poorer and poorer, and more and more weakened in its capacity of production; and that the American War, for a little while, gave, and the various loans, give a show of prosperity to end in greater burdens, and great destruction by famines.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-468"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="468" data-folio="449" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n467_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 449">[p. 449]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">These facts of the insufficiency of the means for the wants go to prove late Lord Lawrence’s statements made in 1864 as Viceroy and in 1873 before the Finance Committee. In 1864 he said that India was on the whole a very poor country and the mass of the people enjoyed only a scanty subsistence; and in 1873 he repeated that the mass of the people of India were so miserably poor that they had barely the means of subsistence, that it was as much as a man could do to feed his family or half feed them, let alone spending money on what might be called luxuries or conveniences. Such then is the manner in which the people of India manage to live; scanty subsistence, and dying away by millions at the very touch of drought. In the case of the Punjab as the latest British possession and least drained, and from other circumstances noted below,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-22-fn-020" href="#sec-22-fn-020" data-fn-target="sec-22-fn-020" role="doc-noteref">*</a></sup> the people have had as yet better resources, in their “Capital” wealth to draw upon. But taking India as a whole, Lord Lawrence’s words are most deplorably but too true.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I need not discuss Mr. Danvers’s paper of 28th June 1880 any further. The fallacy of “other sources,” besides agriculture, mines, manufactures, and foreign trade, pervades his whole argument, and in the latter part of the paper, 2 different matters are mixed up, a little misapprehension has taken place as to my meaning, and some part is irrelevant.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The whole question now before us is simply this.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-469"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="469" data-folio="450" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n468_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 450">[p. 450]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>1st—What the whole actual, material, annual income of India is, as the ultimate balance of all sources and influences, that is available for the use of the whole <em>people of India</em>.</li><li>2nd—What the absolutely necessary wants, and the usual wants of all classes of the people, are; and</li><li>3rd—Whether the income of India is equal to, less, or more than such wants.</li></ul></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">By carefully ascertaining these facts every year, shall we ever be able to know truly, whether India is progressing in prosperity, or sinking in poverty, or is in a stationary condition. This is the whole problem, and it must be boldly faced and clearly answered, if the mission of Britain is the good of India, as I firmly believe it to be.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As to the question, how and by whom, directly or indirectly, the income is actually produced, and how, and by whom, and through what channels, this income is distributed among the whole people, is entirely a different matter, and though important in itself and involving much legislation, is quite separate from the first and fundamental question of the whole total of the means and wants of India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I may explain the misapprehension to which I alluded above. In my tables for consumption, in taking “the cost of absolute necessaries of life of an agricultural labourer,” I meant him, as merely representing the lowest class of labourers of all kinds, so as to show the lowest absolute necessary wants of the people.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am under the impression that there is a Statistical Committee at Calcutta existing for the past 20 years, and I hope it will adopt means to give complete tables of the means and wants of India.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As I am requesting His Lordship, the Secretary of State for India, that Mr. Danvers be asked to work out the means and wants of the people of India during the last 12 years, and that the Government of India may adopt means to perfect the machinery for getting complete information for the future, I submit a few remarks on Mr. Danvers’s tables of 4th January 1879, so kindly sent to me. As I have my Punjab tables only, for comparison, I examine Mr. Danvers’s Punjab tables only.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In his table of quantities of all the inferior grains, Mr. Danvers has taken the crop per acre of only some of the grains whose average is 510 lbs. per acre. But the produce of makai and gram, which are included by Mr. Danvers in the inferior grains, is larger, and the result is a large <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-470" data-page="470" data-folio="451" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n469_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 451">[p. 451]</button> error. The acreage of makai is 1,084,339 acres, and the average produce per acre is 1,500 lbs., so that this produce is under-estimated to the extent of taking only about one-third of the actual quantity. The average produce of gram is 645 lbs. per acre, and the acreage is 2,272,236 acres. On this large acreage, there is nearly 26 per cent. of under-estimate. The result of the whole error in the table of inferior grains is, that the total quantity is taken by Mr. Danvers as 6,504,880,162 lbs., when it actually is 7,371,110,343 lbs., or above 866,200,000 lbs. more.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the prices of inferior grains it is necessary to make proper allowance for the lower prices of such grains as Moth, Kangni, Chìnà, Mater, aad Masur, which are nearly 25 per cent. lower than the other grains— Jowár, Bájrá, Másh, Múng, and Arhar. This makes an over-estimate of £240,000. The price for makái, jow, and gram are given in the report, and separate estimates should, therefore, be made of the values of these grains, to obtain all possible approximation to truth and accuracy.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The total under-estimate by Mr. Danvers is £1,300,000 in the value of inferior grains.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In “other crops,” the value assumed by Mr. Danvers is nearly only one-fourth of what I make, by taking every item separately, <em>i. e.</em> I make Rs. 19,16,294 against Mr. Danvers’s Rs. 4,73,200.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the following articles, Mr. Danvers has adopted the average given in the report, which, as pointed out by me on previous occasions, are taken on the fallacious principle of adding up the produce per acre of the districts and dividing by the number of districts, without any reference to the quantity of acreage of each district.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-131"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-131 · FOLIO 470</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tables/tbl-131_p0470.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tidy/tbl-131_p0470.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col">Produce.</th><th scope="col">Incorrect average.</th><th scope="col">Correct average.</th><th scope="col">Error.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Produce.</th><th scope="col">Incorrect average.</th><th scope="col">Correct average.</th><th scope="col">Correct average.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col">Produce.</th><th scope="col">Incorrect average.</th><th scope="col">Correct average.</th><th scope="col">More per cent.</th><th scope="col">Less per cent.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Vegetables</td><td class="num">4,008</td><td class="num">4,753</td><td class="num">18½</td><td>...</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Sugar<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-22-fn-021" href="#sec-22-fn-021" data-fn-target="sec-22-fn-021" role="doc-noteref">*</a></sup></td><td class="num">449</td><td class="num">646</td><td class="num">44</td><td>...</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Cotton<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-22-fn-021-2" href="#sec-22-fn-021" data-fn-target="sec-22-fn-021" role="doc-noteref">*</a></sup></td><td class="num">102</td><td class="num">105</td><td class="num">3</td><td>...</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Tobacco</td><td class="num">825</td><td class="num">846</td><td class="num">2½</td><td>...</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Fibres</td><td class="num">322</td><td class="num">366</td><td class="num">13¾</td><td>...</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Indigo</td><td class="num">47</td><td class="num">31</td><td>...</td><td class="num">33</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Opium</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">12·5</td><td class="num">25</td><td>...</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-471"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="471" data-folio="452" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n470_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 452">[p. 452]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the case of Indigo, Cotton, Tobacco, and Hemp, the error has not been large, as the incorrect average is adopted by Mr. Danvers for a few districts only I notice such differences, as 2½ and 3 per cent. also, because in dealing with figures of hundreds and thousands of millions, these percentages, singly as well as collectively, seriously disturb the accuracy of results. It is very necessary to avoid, as much as possible, all avoidable errors, large or small, so that then reliance can be placed upon the results.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The report gives the price of 1st sort sugar only, but which, applied to the whole quantity of all kinds, makes the value of nearly ⅔rds of the whole quantity, quite 2½ times greater than it actually is—the over-estimate comes to near £1,800,000.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The price of Indigo, as ascertained by me (Rs. 60 per maund), is nearly 20 per cent. higher than that assumed by Mr. Danvers (Rs. 50 per maund).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Danvers has taken seer=2 lbs. when in reality it is nearly 6 per cent. of a lb. larger, which becomes a serious error in the large amounts to be dealt with.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Danvers has adopted the prices of 1st January 1877 only, instead of taking an average of the prices of the 4 periods given in the Report, to represent the whole year.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In his remarks at page 16, Mr. Danvers makes no allowance for seed, which is an important item. He includes straw, all inferior grains, and cotton seed, and yet makes no allowance at all for the feed of animals, (some 7 millions large cattle and near 4 millions sheep and goats) before apportioning the produce per human head. Grass being not taken, makes some allowance for animals so far.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I cannot say on what grounds, (page 16) 4 per cent. is assumed for annual increase of large cattle and 15 per cent. of sheep and goats. I have not got the report for 1878-9, when the next quinquennial enumeration of stock must have been made, but on comparing the numbers of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-472" data-page="472" data-folio="453" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n471_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 453">[p. 453]</button> the last two enumerations of 1868-9 and 1873-4, the result is as follows:—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-132"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-132 · FOLIO 472</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tables/tbl-132_p0472.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tidy/tbl-132_p0472.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">1868-9.</th><th scope="col">1873-4.</th><th scope="col">Increase.</th><th scope="col">Decrease.</th><th scope="col">Per cent.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Cows, Bullocks and Buffaloes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-22-fn-022" href="#sec-22-fn-022" data-fn-target="sec-22-fn-022" role="doc-noteref">*</a></sup> ... ...</td><td class="num">6,797,561</td><td class="num">6,570,212</td><td>...</td><td class="num">227,349</td><td class="num">3 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Horses ... ... ...</td><td class="num">96,226</td><td class="num">84,639</td><td>...</td><td class="num">11,587</td><td class="num">12</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ponies ... ... ...</td><td class="num">51,302</td><td class="num">51,395</td><td class="num">93</td><td>...</td><td>...</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Donkeys ... ... ...</td><td class="num">257,615</td><td class="num">288,118</td><td class="num">30,503</td><td>...</td><td class="num">11.8</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Camels ... ... ...</td><td class="num">148,582</td><td class="num">165,567</td><td class="num">16,985</td><td>...</td><td class="num">11.4</td></tr><tr class="row-subtotal"><td>Total ... ... ...</td><td colspan="2">7,351,286 — 7,159,931</td><td>=</td><td class="num">191,355</td><td></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Sheep and Goats ...</td><td class="num">3,803,819</td><td class="num">3,849,842</td><td class="num">46,023</td><td>...</td><td class="num">1 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>5</sub></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">From this comparison, it appears, that in the important items of cows, bullocks and buffaloes, instead of any increase, there is actually decrease of 227,349 or 3 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> per cent. during the 5 years. In horses also, there is a decrease of about 2 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> per cent. every year instead of 4 per cent. increase. In ponies the increase is hardly <sup>1</sup>/<sub>5</sub> per cent. in 5 years, in donkeys about 11 per cent., and in camels about 11 per cent., in all the 5 years, or about 2 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub> per cent. per year instead of 4 per cent. In sheep and goats, the increase is hardly 1 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>5</sub> per cent. in 5 years instead of 15 per cent. per year. For cows and bullocks, and sheep and goats, there is one allowance to be made, viz. for what are killed for food. To make out the increase in cows, &amp;c. of 4 per cent. every year, nearly 4 <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> per cent. must have been killed every year for food, and for sheep and goats the percentage of killed should be nearly 14 <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> per cent. per annum. Is it so?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Danvers has assumed ghi, produced in the Punjab, as 4 times as much as imported (52,303 maunds) into it, and he thus makes the quantity produced to be 209,212 maunds. Now the value of the imported ghi is also given in the report, as Rs. 9,64,028 which, taken 4 times, would be £385,611. But Mr. Danvers has overlooked this actual price and adopted the fallacious average of the table of prices in the report, which makes the price 1<em>s</em>. 12<em>c</em>. per Rupee. At this incorrect price, the value will be £478,198, or nearly 25 per cent. more than <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-473" data-page="473" data-folio="454" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n472_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 454">[p. 454]</button> the actual value given in the report. But not only has there been this incorrect increase thus made, but by some arithmetical mistake, the value put down by Mr. Danvers, is above 3 times as much as even this increased amount, <em>i. e.</em> instead of £478,198, Mr. Danvers has put down £1,501,096. If this be not merely an arithmetical mistake, it requires explanation.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Danvers has taken the import of ghi from “foreign trade” only, and has overlooked a further quantity of import “inter-provincially” of 16,312 maunds of the value of £34,741, which, taken 4 times, would be £138,964, making up the total value of the assumed produce of ghi in the Punjab to be £385,611 + 138,964 = £524,575.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Working upon Mr. Danvers’s own assumption, and what information I have been at present able to obtain, it appears that the assumption of 4 times the import—or £525,000, will be an under-estimate by a good deal. I am not at present able to test the accuracy of Mr. Danvers’s assumption of the produce of milk, nor of the information I am using below, but I give it just as I have it, to illustrate the principle. I adopt Mr. Danvers’s assumption of 10 per cent. of the whole cattle to be milch animals. The number then will be 657,000. Of these, cows may be taken, I am told by a Punjabee, at 75 per cent., and buffaloes 25 per cent. This will give 164,250 buffaloes and 492,750 cows. Each buffalo may be taken, on an average, as giving 6 seers of milk per day for 6 months in the year, and each cow about 3 seers. The Mr. Danvers assumes for milk used in the province to be about Rs. 10 per annum from each of the 10 per cent. of the cattle, and taking the price of milk to be 16 seers per Rupee, the quantity of milk used would be 657,000 × 160 = 105,120,000 seers. This, deducted from the above total produce of milk, will give (443,475,000—105,120,000) 338,355,000 seers as converted into ghi. The produce of ghi is about <sup>1</sup>/<sub>5</sub> to <sup>1</sup>/<sub>10</sub> of milk, according to quality. Assuming <sup>1</sup>/<sub>12</sub> as the average, the total quantity of ghi will be about 28,196,250 seers = 704,906 maunds, or allowing a little for wastage, say 700,000 maunds, which; at the import price (Rs. 13,11,445 for 68,615 maunds) of Rs. 19 per maund, will give about £1,339,300, or nearly 2¾ times as much as Mr. Danvers <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-474" data-page="474" data-folio="455" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n473_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 455">[p. 455]</button> has assumed. I have endeavoured in a hurry to get this information as well as I could, but it can be obtained correctly by the officials on the spot. My object, at present, was simply to show that, calculated on Mr. Danvers’s assumption of milch-cattle and milk used, how much ghi should be produced in the country, if the information I have used be correct.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-133"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-133 · FOLIO 473</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tables/tbl-133_p0473.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/essays-speeches-addresses-writings-naoroji-1887/datasets/tidy/tbl-133_p0473.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>quantity of milk will then be 164,250 × seers 6 × days 180 =</td><td class="num">177,390,000</td><td>seers.</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>492,750 × 3 × 180 =</td><td class="num">266,085,000</td><td>„</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td>Total ..</td><td class="num">443,475,000</td><td>„</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For hides and skins, the export only is taken into account, but a quantity must be consumed in the province itself, which requires to be added.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The value assumed, Rs. 100 per horse, is rather too high. Rs. 60 or 70, I am told, would be fairer, so also for ponies, Rs. 25 to 30 instead of Rs. 35, and camels, Rs. 60 or 70 or 75 instead of Rs. 100. For sheep, &amp;c. Rs. 1½ instead of Re. 1, would be fairer.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But as I have said above, officials in India can give all this inform a tion correctly for every year, and I do not see any reason why this should not be done. I urgently repeat my request, that the wants and means of the last 12 or 15 years may be ordered by His Lordship, the Secretary of State, to be carefully worked out, as far as practicable, and that future reports should be required to give complete information.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Railways</span>.</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I may take railways to represent Public Works. The benefits generally derived from Railways are these. They distribute the produce of the country from parts where it is produced or is in abundance, to the parts where it is wanted, so that no part of the produce is wasted, which, otherwise, would be the case if no facility of communication existed. In thus utilising the whole produce of the country, the railway becomes directly a saving agent, and indirectly, thereby, helps in increasing the production of the country.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It brings the produce to the ports at the least possible cost for exportation and commercial competition for foreign trade, and thus indirectly helps in obtaining the profits of foreign trade, which are an increase to the annual income of a country.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Every country in building railways, even by borrowed capital, derives the benefit of a large portion of such borrowed capital, as the capital of the country, which indirectly helps in increasing the production of the country. Excepting interest paid for such borrowed capital to the foreign lending country, the rest of the whole income remains <em>in the country.</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-475" data-page="475" data-folio="456" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n474_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 456">[p. 456]</button> But the result of <em>all</em> the above benefits from railways, is ultimately realised and comprised in the actual annual income of the country.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The misfortune of India is that she does not derive the above benefits as every other country does.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">You build a railway in England and, say, its gross income is a million. All the employès, from the Chairman down to the common labourer, <em>are Englishmen</em>. Every farthing that is spent from the gross income, is so much returned to Englishmen, as direct maintenance to so many people of <em>England</em>, and to England at large as a part of its general wealth. Whether the shareholders get their 5 per cent. or 10 per cent. or 1 per cent. or 0 per cent. or even lose, it matters not at all to the whole country. Every farthing of the income of the million is fully and solely enjoyed by <em>the people of the country</em>—excepting only (if you borrowed a portion of the capital from foreign parts) the interest you may pay for such loan. But such interest forms a small portion of the whole income, and every country with good railways, can very well afford to pay. All the benefits of railways are thus obtained and enjoyed by <em>the people of the country</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Take the case of the United States. India and the States are both borrowers for their railways (the latter only partially), and they both pay interest to the lending countries. They both buy, say, their rails, machinery, &amp;c. from England, the States buying only a portion. So far they are under somewhat similar circumstances. But here the parallel ends. In the United States, every cent of the income of the railway (excepting the interest on the Foreign loan) is the income <em>of the people of the country</em>—is a direct maintenance for the people employed on it, and an indirect property of the whole country and remaining <em>in</em> it.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In India the case is quite different. First, for the Directors’ home establishments, Government Superintendence, and what not in England, a portion of the income must go from India; then a large European staff of employés (excepting only for inferior and lowest places or work left for natives) must eat up and take away another large portion of the income, and to the rest, the people of the country are welcome, with the result, that out of their production which they give to the railways, only a <em>portion</em> returns to them and <em>not the whole</em>, as in all other countries (except interest on foreign loan); and the diminution lessens so far the capacity of production every year. Such expenditure, both in England and India, is so much direct deprivation of the natural maintenance of as many people of India, of similar classes, and a loss to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-476" data-page="476" data-folio="457" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/essaysspeechesa00paregoog/page/n475_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 457">[p. 457]</button> the general wealth and means of the people at large.
